Xinyu Su
a

Abstract




Equidistribution of mesh patterns of short length

Abstract.

We study the equidistribution of mesh patterns of length 2. We show that the number of equidistribution equivalence classes lies between 105 and 108, and conjecture that it is exactly 105. As a consequence, we obtain an upper bound of 49 Wilf-classes, improving the previously known bound of 56 due to Hilmarsson et al., and reducing the problem to three remaining conjectural equivalences (with the actual number conjectured to be 46).

Our approach combines bijective constructions, generating functions, recurrence relations, and structural symmetries. We establish several new equidistribution results, including four previously unknown distribution classes, connect numerous patterns to known distributions in the literature, and resolve seven open pattern-avoidance enumeration problems posed by Hilmarsson et al.

This work provides a near-complete classification of mesh patterns of length 2 and unifies several previously isolated results within a coherent framework.

Keywords:
mesh pattern, distribution, avoidance, bijection, generating function

AMS Subject Classifications:
05A05, 05A15.

1
Introduction

Denote by
S_{n}
the set of permutations of
[n]:=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, and let
\varepsilon
be the empty permutation. We call permutations of length
n

n
-permutations. A
pattern
is a permutation. A permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
avoids a pattern
p=p_{1}p_{2}\cdots p_{k}\in S_{k}
if there is no subsequence
\pi_{i_{1}}\pi_{i_{2}}\cdots\pi_{i_{k}}
such that
\pi_{i_{j}}<\pi_{i_{m}}
if and only if
p_{j}<p_{m}
. For example, the permutation
32154
avoids the pattern
231
.

The notion of a
mesh pattern
, generalizing several classes of patterns, was introduced by Brändén and Claesson
[
1
]
to provide explicit expansions for certain permutation statistics as, possibly infinite, linear combinations of (classical) permutation patterns.
A pair
(\tau,R)
, where
\tau
is a permutation of length
k
and
R
is a subset of
[0,k]\times[0,k]
, where

[0,k]
denotes the interval of the integers from
0
to
k
, is a
mesh pattern of length
k
.
Let
({i,j})
denote the box whose corners have coordinates
(i,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1)
, and
(i+1,j)
. Let the horizontal lines represent the values, and the vertical lines denote the positions in the pattern. Mesh patterns can be drawn by shading the boxes in
R
. The picture

represents the mesh pattern with
\tau=132
and
R=\{({0,0}),({1,2}),({2,1}),({2,3}),({3,0}),({3,1})\}
.

A subsequence
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{i_{1}}\pi_{i_{2}}\cdots\pi_{i_{k}}
of a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}
is an occurrence of a mesh pattern
(\tau,R)
if (a)
\pi^{\prime}
is order-isomorphic to
\tau
, and (b) the shaded squares specified by
R
contain no elements of
\pi
other than those in
\pi^{\prime}
.

Let
S_{n}(p)
denote the set of
p
-avoiding
n
-permutations, and let
s_{n}(p)=|S_{n}(p)|
. For two patterns
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, if
s_{n}(p_{1})=s_{n}(p_{2})
for all
n\geq 0
, then
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are
Wilf-equivalent
. Wilf-equivalence classes are also referred to here as
Wilf-classes
. Define
s_{n,k}(p)
as the number of
n
-permutations containing exactly
k
occurrences of a pattern
p
. If
s_{n,k}(p_{1})=s_{n,k}(p_{2})
for all
n,k\geq 0
, then
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are
equidistributed
(or
equivalent
), which we denote by
p_{1}\sim p_{2}
. The patterns
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are said to be
jointly equidistributed
if, for all
n\geq 0
, the number of
n
-permutations with
k
occurrences of
p_{1}
and
\ell
occurrences of
p_{2}
is equal to the number of
n
-permutations with
\ell
occurrences of
p_{1}
and
k
occurrences of
p_{2}
.

Hilmarsson et al.
[
4
]
initiated a systematic study of Wilf-equivalences and the enumeration of permutations avoiding mesh patterns of length 2. As a result of their work, they proved that there are at most 56 Wilf-classes for these patterns and conjectured that there are in fact only 46 such classes. In Table
1
, we present the conjectured Wilf-equivalences from
[
4
]
. As a result of our study, we strengthen the results of Hilmarsson et al. by proving the following theorem and conjecturing that all previously conjectured Wilf-equivalences are indeed equidistributions of patterns.

Theorem 1.1
.

The number of Wilf-equivalence classes for mesh patterns of length
2
is at most
49
.

Proof.

From
[
4
]
, the number of Wilf-classes is known to be at most 56. Using our numbering of pattern classes, some of the conjectured Wilf-classes in
[
4
]
are Classes 36, 46, 47, 56, and 70. These classes have now been proved to be equidistributed (a stronger result than Wilf-equivalence): Classes 36 and 46 are established in Theorems
4.1
and
6.1
of this paper, Classes 47 and 70 in
[
3
]
, and Class 56 in
[
5
]
. Since only three pairs of patterns with conjectured Wilf-equivalences (in fact, conjectured equidistributions) remain, namely, Classes 54, 69, and 71 in Table
1
, the number of Wilf-classes is at most 49.
∎

nr.

repr.
p

ref.

nr.

repr.
p

ref.

47

Table 1:
Conjectured Wilf-equivalences in Tables 11 and 12 of
[
4
]
, which have now been proved as equidistributions or remain conjectured equidistributions. Next to each pattern in the table, we give the original number of the pattern class in
[
4
]
.

Additionally, as consequences of our enumerative results in this paper, we solve seven open pattern-avoidance enumeration problems from
[
4
]
. In particular, Theorems
4.1
,
7.1
, and
7.2
resolve patterns labelled in
[
4
]
as follows: patterns 55 and 56 (corresponding to our Class 36), patterns 48–50 (Class 47), and patterns 51 and 52 (Class 54).

The results in
[
4
]
were extended to a systematic study of distributions of mesh patterns of length 2 by Kitaev and Zhang in
[
5
]
. In particular, the authors of
[
5
]
obtained 27 distribution results for these patterns, thereby resolving some of the avoidance problems posed in
[
4
]
. Some of the equidistribution conjectures from
[
5
]
were settled by Han and Zeng in
[
3
]
, while two cases remain open. We summarize the known equidistribution results in Table
2
. The study of distributions of patterns of longer lengths can be found in
[
2
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
]
, with the main focus on the equidistribution of mesh patterns of length 3.

As the main result of this paper, we provide a nearly complete classification of equidistribution equivalence classes for mesh patterns of length 2, showing that the number of these classes is at most 108 and at least 105. We conjecture that this number is in fact 105. Therefore, apart from two known equidistribution conjectures, we propose one additional conjecture (see Table
5
). Resolving these three conjectures would settle the open problem that the number of Wilf-classes for these patterns is 46; see Table
1
.

To obtain these results, we construct four involved bijections, use several simpler bijections and symmetries, apply generating functions and recurrence relations, and exploit known results from the literature. Our new enumerative contributions include four previously unknown distributions (Classes 15, 25, 73, and 80), as well as non-trivial connections between many patterns and known distributions from
[
5
,
6
]
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we provide definitions, preliminary observations, and notation, together with known enumerative results, some of which are used later in the paper, while others are included to make the exposition self-contained; the known enumerative results are summarized in Table
6
. In Section
3
, we present straightforward equidistributions, which are summarized in Table
3
. Equidistributions explained via generating functions and recurrence relations are discussed in Sections
4
and
5
, respectively. In Section
6
, we present four involved bijections explaining additional equidistributions.

In Section
7
, we enumerate avoidance classes corresponding to some equivalence classes arising from trivial bijections; these are presented in Table
7
in Appendix
A
. This section also contains two additional enumerative results not related to Table
7
. Finally, in Section
8
, we provide concluding remarks, and in Appendix
B
, we present the distribution of each class of patterns on
n
-permutations for
1\leq n\leq 7
. This appendix both justifies the lower bound of 105 equivalence classes and provides data for researchers wishing to further investigate distributions of mesh patterns of length 2.

2
Preliminaries

For a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
, the entry
\pi_{i}
is called a
left-to-right maximum
(resp.,
left-to-right minimum
) if
\pi_{i}>\pi_{j}
(resp.,
\pi_{i}<\pi_{j}
) for all
j<i
. Similarly,
\pi_{i}
is called a
right-to-left maximum
(resp.,
right-to-left minimum
) if
\pi_{i}>\pi_{j}
(resp.,
\pi_{i}<\pi_{j}
) for all
j>i
. In particular,
\pi_{1}
is both a left-to-right maximum and a left-to-right minimum, while
\pi_{n}
is both a right-to-left maximum and a right-to-left minimum. Let

F(x):=\sum_{n\geq 0}n!\,x^{n}.

This function appears frequently in our enumerative results. Throughout the paper, we use
A(x)
(resp.,
F(x,q)
) to denote the ordinary generating function for permutations avoiding the pattern in question (resp., the generating function for the distribution of occurrences of the pattern). Hence, if
p(\pi)
denotes the number of occurrences of a pattern
p
in a permutation
\pi
, then

F(x,q)=\sum_{n\geq 0}x^{n}\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}q^{p(\pi)},

and
A(x)=F(x,0)
. We abbreviate “generating function” as g.f.

We establish many equidistribution results using three fundamental symmetry operations on mesh patterns:
complement
(vertical reflection),
reverse
(horizontal reflection), and
inverse
(reflection in the line
y=x
). For a mesh pattern
p=(\tau,R)
of length
m
, define

\displaystyle p^{c}

\displaystyle=(\tau^{c},R^{c}),

\displaystyle\tau^{c}(i)

\displaystyle=m+1-\tau(i),

\displaystyle R^{c}

\displaystyle=\{(x,\,m-y):(x,y)\in R\},

\displaystyle p^{r}

\displaystyle=(\tau^{r},R^{r}),

\displaystyle\tau^{r}(i)

\displaystyle=m+1-\tau(m+1-i),

\displaystyle R^{r}

\displaystyle=\{(m-x,\,y):(x,y)\in R\},

\displaystyle p^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\tau^{-1},R^{-1}),

\displaystyle\tau^{-1}(i)

\displaystyle=j\text{ where }\tau(j)=i,

\displaystyle R^{-1}

\displaystyle=\{(y,\,x):(x,y)\in R\}.

It is easy to see that if a pattern
p_{1}
is obtained from a pattern
p_{2}
by applying any of these three operations, then
p_{1}\sim p_{2}
. In Tables
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
, and
7
, equivalences arising from these operations are indicated by placing the corresponding patterns in the same cell. We refer to such equivalences as
trivial
and do not discuss them further.

Table 2:
Known equidistributions.

Throughout the paper, we frequently use the insertion of elements into an
(n-1)
-permutation
\pi^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\pi^{\prime}_{n-1}
to obtain an
n
-permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}
. The insertion of the new largest element is straightforward. Inserting the new smallest element amounts to replacing each
\pi^{\prime}_{i}
by
\pi^{\prime}_{i}+1
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
. More generally, when inserting a new element
x
either on the left or on the right, we increase by
1
all entries of
\pi^{\prime}
that are greater than or equal to
x
.

Table 6:
Known enumerative results for all equivalence classes. “A” denotes known avoidance enumeration, and “D” denotes known distribution. For the reader’s convenience, non-trivial known distributions from the literature are also presented in this paper. We note that, by the Shading Lemma in
[
4
]
, the following sets of classes have identical avoidance sets:
\{5,99\}
,
\{23,58\}
,
\{29,41,88\}
,
\{30,60\}
, and
\{59,82\}
. Hence, the table presents 19 open avoidance problems and 72 open distribution problems.

The following theorem is used frequently in our paper.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
5
]
)
.

For the pattern

(equivalently,

), we have

A(x)=\frac{F(x)}{1+xF(x)};\quad F(x,q)=\frac{F(x)}{1+x(1-q)F(x)}.

The following enumerative results are known.

Theorem 2.2

(
[
5
]
)
.

For the patterns in Class 56 (represented by

,

,

),

\displaystyle A(x)=\frac{2F(x)-1}{F(x)};\ \ \ \ \ F(x,q)=\frac{(2-q)F(x)+q-1}{(1-q)F(x)+q}.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
5
]
)
.

For any pattern
p
in Class 61 (represented by

and

),

\displaystyle s_{n,k}(p)=

\displaystyle\,s_{n-1,k-1}(p)+(k+1)s_{n-1,k+1}(p)+(n-k-1)s_{n-1,k}(p)

with the initial conditions
s_{1,0}(p)=1
,
s_{2,0}(p)=1
, and
s_{2,1}(p)=1
.

\displaystyle\quad+(k+1)s_{n-2,k+1}(p)+(n-2k-2)s_{n-2,k}(p)-(n-k-1)s_{n-2,k-1}(p)

.

A
descent
in a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}
is a position
i
such that
\pi_{i}>\pi_{i+1}
. The distribution of descents is clearly equivalent to the distribution of the pattern

,
and is given by the
Eulerian numbers
. We state this classical result in the following theorem.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
11
]
)
.

For
n\geq 1
and
0\leq k\leq n-1
, the number of permutations in
S_{n}
with exactly
k
occurrences of
any pattern
p
in Class
74
is given by

s_{n,k}(p)=\sum_{j=0}^{k+1}(-1)^{j}\binom{n+1}{j}(k+1-j)^{n}.

and discuss the corresponding enumerative results. We note that the proof of the following lemma in fact establishes the joint equidistribution of the patterns in question.

Lemma 3.1
.

There are two classes of equidistributed mesh patterns, shown in Figure

, where each of
x_{1},\dots,x_{6}
takes one of two values: shaded or unshaded.

(that is, we keep the position of
n
fixed and complement the remaining elements). Clearly, this map establishes the left equivalence in Figure

The right equivalence in Figure

follows by reversing the elements of
\pi
to the left of
n
.∎

p

(n-1)!

.

and

, while the number of ways to arrange the

in their fixed positions is
k!
.
Thus, for

s_{n,k}(p)=k!(n-k-2)!\sum_{i=0}^{n-k-2}\binom{i+k}{i}\,(n-k-1-i).

After some algebraic manipulations, we obtain (

Theorem 3.11
.

The patterns in Class
62
are equivalent. Also, for
n\geq 1
, there are
(n-1)!(n-H_{n-1})

n
-permutations avoiding any pattern in this class, where
H_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k}
is the
n
-th Harmonic number.

Proof.

The equivalence follows from Lemma
3.1
applied to

,

,

, and

.

Next, we enumerate the avoiders of

.
Let
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
.
If
\pi_{1}=n
, then
\pi
cannot contain an occurrence of

.
On the other hand, if
\pi_{1}\neq n
and
\pi
contains an occurrence of the pattern, then it is easy to see that
\pi_{1}n
must form such an occurrence. In this case, the elements
\pi_{1}+1,\pi_{1}+2,\ldots,n-1
must all lie between
\pi_{1}
and
n
in
\pi
. The number of choices for such a permutation is
\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}i!(n-i-2)!{n-1\choose i+1}
,
where
(n-i-2)!
counts the number of ways to arrange the elements smaller than
\pi_{1}
, and the binomial coefficient counts the choices of positions for the elements larger than
\pi_{1}
in
\pi
. Therefore, the number of
n
-permutations with
\pi_{1}\neq n
that avoid

is

n!-(n-1)!-\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}i!(n-i-2)!{n-1\choose i+1}.

Adding the
(n-1)!
avoiders with
\pi_{1}=n
, we obtain the desired result.
∎

Only part of the proof of the next theorem admits an “easily explainable equidistribution”; nevertheless, we present the proof in full in this section rather than splitting it into separate parts. Also note that Theorem
5.1
below gives yet another connection to the unsigned Stirling number of the first kind.

Theorem 3.13
.

The patterns in Class
68
are equivalent. For any pattern

s_{n,k}(p)=s_{n-1,k-1}(p)+(n-1)s_{n-1,k}(p)

(2)

and

We complete the proof by showing that the distribution of the patterns

The pattern

.

\pi
avoids the pattern. It follows that
s_{n,0}(p)=(n-1)!
. Now construct
\pi
by inserting the new minimum element into
\pi^{\prime}
. Clearly, the number of occurrences of
p
in
\pi^{\prime}
cannot decrease. Moreover, the only insertion that increases the number of occurrences of

The pattern

.
Clearly, if

p

). We show that the distribution of

is the same. We claim that

F(x)=A(x)+xA(x)(F(x)-1).

(6)

Indeed, each permutation (counted by
F(x)
on the LHS) either avoids the pattern (and hence is counted by
A(x)
) or contains it. Among all occurrences
ab
of the pattern in
\pi
(
b=a+1
), select the one with the smallest possible
a
, as shown in Figure
4
. Then the block
A
must avoid

, while
BbC
can be any non-empty permutation counted by
F(x)-1
. Note that
\pi
is uniquely determined by
A
and
BbC
, and the element
a
contributes a factor of
x
. This yields (
6
), and the formula for
A(x)
given in (
5
). For the distribution, we have

F(x,q)=A(x)+xqA(x)(F(x,q)-1),

(7)

whose derivation follows the same lines as in the case of avoidance. We note that each occurrence of the pattern in
BbC
is also an occurrence in
\pi
, and the factor of
q
corresponds to the occurrence
ab
of the pattern. The formula for
F(x,q)
follows from (
7
), and it is given by (
5
).
∎

5
Equidistributions by recurrence relations

The following theorem provides another connection to the unsigned Stirling numbers of the first kind (such a connection was already observed in Theorem
3.13
).

Theorem 5.1
.

The patterns in Class
15
are equivalent. For any pattern
p
in the class,

s_{n,k}(p)=s_{n-1,k}(p)+(n-1)s_{n-1,k-1}(p)

(8)

with the initial conditions
s_{0,0}(p)=s_{1,0}=1
and
s_{1,1}(p)=0
, which shows that
s_{n,k}(p)=C(n,n-k)
, the unsigned Stirling number of the first kind. The bivariate exponential g.f. for
s_{n,k}(p)
is given by

\sum_{n\geq 0}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{x^{n}}{n!}q^{k}=(1-xq)^{-1/q}.

Proof.

It easy to see that for both patterns,

and

, decreasing permutations are the only one avoiding them.

Now, for the pattern

, inserting the new minimal element to create a larger permutation never destroys any existing occurrence of the pattern, does not change the number of occurrences if inserted at the end, and introduces precisely one new occurrence of the pattern (formed by the new minimum element and the closest right-to-left maximum to its right) if inserted in a position other than the rightmost one. This explains (
8
).

However, for the pattern

, exactly the same occurs when inserting the new minimum element, except that the new occurrence of the pattern is formed by the new minimum element and the element next to it on its right. Thus,

6
Equidistributions by bijections

In this section, we present four bijections. Each bijection
f
is in fact an involution, that is,
f(f(\pi))=\pi
, and proves a stronger statement than mere equidistribution, namely the joint equidistribution of the patterns involved, since occurrences of the patterns in question are mapped directly to one another.

.

Let
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
, and let
r_{1}>r_{2}>\cdots>r_{\ell}
be the sequence of right-to-left maxima of
\pi
. Let
R=\{r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{\ell}\}
. Note that if
ab
is an occurrence of either pattern, then
b=r_{i}
for some
i
, and
a
is immediately to the left of
r_{i}
. Also, each
r_{i}
can be involved in at most one occurrence of

or

.

The transformation
f:\mathcal{S}_{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{n}
is obtained by considering
r_{i}
, for
i=\ell,\ell-1,\ldots,1
, one at a time. For each
r_{i}
, let
x_{i}
be the element immediately to the left of
r_{i}
, if it exists. If
x_{i}\in R
, or if
r_{i}=\pi_{1}
, we do nothing. Otherwise, we define the set
S_{i}
as:

S_{i}:=\{y\mid y\text{ is to the left of }r_{i},\text{ and }y<r_{i}\}.

Note that by definition,
x_{i}\in S_{i}
. Let the elements of
S_{i}
be ordered as
a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{j}
, where
j=|S_{i}|\geq 1
. We perform the following steps:

•

If
x_{i}r_{i}
is not an occurrence of

or

, or if it is involved in occurrences of both these patterns, we leave
\pi
unchanged and proceed to
r_{i-1}
.

•

If
x_{i}r_{i}
is an occurrence of

but not of

, then
a_{j}=x_{i}
, and we modify the elements of
S_{i}
in
\pi
by the following cyclic shift:

x_{i}\to a_{1},\quad a_{1}\to a_{2},\quad a_{2}\to a_{3},\quad\dots,\quad a_{j-2}\to a_{j-1},\quad a_{j-1}\to x_{i}.

After this shift,
a_{1}r_{i}
becomes an occurrence of the pattern

, and we lost the occurrence
x_{i}r_{i}
of the pattern

in
\pi
.

•

If
x_{i}r_{i}
is an occurrence of

but not

, then
a_{1}=x_{i}
, and we modify the elements of
S_{i}
in
\pi
by the substitution:

x_{i}\to a_{j},\quad a_{j}\to a_{j-1},\quad a_{j-1}\to a_{j-2},\quad\dots,\quad a_{2}\to a_{1},\quad a_{1}\to x_{i}

This transformation maps the occurrence
x_{i}r_{i}
of

to an occurrence
a_{j}r_{i}
of

.

1

Figure 5:
The steps of implementing the mapping
f
in the proof of Theorem
6.1
showing that
f(23471856)=23457816
.

The steps of
f
are illustrated in the example in Figure
5
. Note that in that example, the occurrence 56 of

(resp., occurrence 18 of

) becomes the occurrence 16 of

(resp., occurrence 78 of

).

We need to justify that a cyclic shift at each step cannot introduce or destroy any occurrences of the patterns other than those already discussed. This will show that
f
is an involution since the cyclic shifts described above are inverses of each other.

First note that the transformation
f
acts strictly on the values within the set
S_{i}
.

•

In the case when
x_{i}r_{i}
is an occurrence of

but not of

, there must be a unique element
a_{i}\in S_{i}
such that
a_{1}\leq a_{i}<x_{i}
. After modification,
x_{i}
becomes the smallest element in
S_{i}
,
a_{1}r_{i}
is an occurrence of

but not of

, and there is no occurrence of

involving
r_{i}
because of the element
a_{i+1}
.

Now, if
x_{j}r_{j}
is an occurrence of the pattern

(resp.,

) to the right of
r_{i}
, then there are no elements between
x_{j}
and
r_{j}
(resp., less than
x_{j}
) to the left of
x_{j}
. Since the cyclic shift does not change the set
S_{i}
(it only reorders the elements of
S_{i}
in
\pi
),
x_{j}r_{j}
remains an occurrence of the same pattern.

Finally, suppose that
x_{j}r_{j}
is an occurrence of either of the patterns to the left of
r_{i}
, that is,
r_{j}>r_{i}
. If
x_{j}>r_{i}
, then clearly after the cyclic shift
x_{j}r_{j}
remains an occurrence of the same pattern. On the other hand, if
x_{j}<r_{i}
, then we must have
x_{j}<x_{i}
(otherwise,
x_{i}r_{i}
cannot be an occurrence of

). In this case, the cyclic shift moves
x_{j}
and all elements to its left, if any, upward, and it does not introduce any elements between
x_{j}
and
r_{j}
to the left of
x_{j}
. Moreover, no elements less than
x_{j}
can appear to its left if there were none in
\pi
. Hence,
x_{j}r_{j}
remains an occurrence of the same pattern.

•

The case when
x_{i}r_{i}
is an occurrence of

but not of

can be analyzed similarly to the previous case and is therefore omitted.

The theorem is proved.
∎

Theorem 6.2
.

The patterns in Class
49
are equivalent.

Proof.

We will explain bijectively that
\raisebox{2.58334pt}{

{}{{}}{}

Let
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
, and write
\pi=A_{1}r_{1}A_{2}r_{2}\cdots A_{\ell}r_{\ell}
, where
r_{1}>r_{2}>\cdots>r_{\ell}
is the sequence of right-to-left maxima of
\pi
. Note that if
ab
is an occurrence of either pattern, then
b=r_{i}
for some
i
, and
a
must lie in
A_{i}
. Clearly, each
r_{i}
can be involved in at most one occurrence of

or

.

The transformation
f:\mathcal{S}_{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{n}
is obtained by considering
r_{i}
, for
i=\ell,\ell-1,\ldots,1
, one at a time. If
r_{i}
, is not involved in an occurrence of either pattern, or if
r_{i}
is involved in occurrence of both patterns, do not change anything and proceed with
r_{i-1}
. Otherwise, we define the set
M_{i}
as:

M_{i}:=\{y\mid y\text{ is to the left of }r_{i-1},\text{ and }y<r_{i}\}.

By definition,
M_{1}=\emptyset
. Let the elements of
M_{i}
be ordered as
a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{j}
, where
j=|M_{i}|
. We perform the following steps if
j\geq 1
(if
j=0
, we do nothing):

•

Suppose that
ar_{i}
is an occurrence of

, but there is no occurrence
br_{i}
of

. We modify
\pi
by performing the following substitutions:

a\to a_{1},\,a_{1}\to a_{2},\,a_{2}\to a_{3},\,\dots,\,a_{j-1}\to a_{j},\,a_{j}\to a.

Then
ar_{i}
becomes an occurrence of

.

•

Suppose that
a{r_{i}}
is an occurrence of

, but there is no occurrence

involving
r_{i}
. We modify
\pi
by the following substitution:

a\to a_{j},\,a_{j}\to a_{j-1},\,\dots,\,a_{2}\to a_{1},\,a_{1}\to a.

Then
ar_{i}
becomes an occurrence of

.

The steps of
f
are illustrated in the example in Figure
6
. Note that in that example,
\pi
has two occurrence of

(48 and 67) and two occurrence of

(15 and 28), and
f(\pi)
has two occurrence of

(28 and 17) and two occurrence of

(45 and 68).

The steps in implementing the mapping
f
in the proof of Theorem
6.2
, showing that

. The middle two diagrams represent the same permutation, and the rectangles indicate the regions in which the elements are moved by the cyclic shifts.

One can justify that a cyclic shift at each step cannot introduce or destroy any occurrences of the patterns, other than those already discussed, in an analogous way to the proof of Theorem
6.1
. The operations are very similar; the only difference is that, in Theorem

, the cyclic shift is applied to a larger block extending to the region between
r_{i-1}
and
r_{i}
. We omit further details. The proof is complete.

\displaystyle s_{n,k}(p)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left[i\cdot s_{n-1,k-1,i}(p)+(n-i)\cdot s_{n-1,k,i}(p)\right]

with the initial conditions

,

for all

, where

occurrences of

and

\displaystyle s_{n,k,\ell}(p)=s_{n-1,k,\ell-1}(p)+\ell\cdot s_{n-1,k-1,\ell}(p)+(n-1-\ell)\cdot s_{n-1,k,\ell}(p)

with the initial conditions

,

for all
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
,

if

, and

Let
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
and
r_{1}>r_{2}>\cdots>r_{\ell}
be the sequence of right-to-left maxima in
\pi
, which define the areas
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{\ell}
as shown in Figure
7
. Keeping the values and positions of
r_{i}
’s, we modify
\pi
by reversing the elements in each block
A_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq\ell
, while keeping the positions of the elements in
A_{j}
. Let
\sigma
be the resulting permutation. That is, if
\pi_{i_{1}}\pi_{i_{2}}\ldots\pi_{i_{m}}
,
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{m}
is a subsequence of
\pi
constituting the block
A_{j}
, then the elements in
\sigma
in positions
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{m}
will be, respectively,
\pi_{i_{m}},\pi_{i_{m-1}},\ldots,\pi_{i_{1}}
. See Figure
8
for an example.

Note that in the example in Figure
8
, the occurrences of

(7(10) and 9(10) in the top block, and 15, 35, and 45 in the bottom block) and the occurrences of

(8(10) and 9(10) in the top block, and 25 and 45 in the bottom block) in
\pi
are mapped to one another. We will next prove that this is not a coincidence; under the bijection, if
ab
is an occurrence of

in
\pi
then
ab
is an occurrence of

in
\sigma
, and vice versa.

Note that if
ab
is an occurrence of either pattern, then
b=r_{i}
for some
i
. Due to the shading of square (2,1), it follows that
a
must belong to
A_{i}
for that
i
. Moreover, since there is no shading in squares (0,0), (0,2), (1,0), (1,2), and (2,0) in both patterns, the blocks
A_{j}
are independent in the sense that reversing the elements in any block cannot affect (create or destroy an occurrence of either of the patterns) another block. But then, we can consider each block independently, and we are done since clearly reversing elements inside the block interchanges occurrences of

and

, and hence the respective occurrences of

and

in
\pi
.

, which is equivalent to

by taking the inverse. The initial conditions for
s_{n,k}(p)
and
s_{n,k,\ell}(p)
are easy to check. To explain (
10
), we consider generating
\pi\in S_{n}
by inserting the new smallest element 1 in a permutation
\pi^{\prime}\in S_{n-1}
. Note that if
ab
is an occurrence of
p
in
\pi^{\prime}
, then
b=r_{i}
for some
i
, where
r_{i}
is a right-to-left maximum in
\pi^{\prime}
. In (
10
):

•

s_{n-1,k,\ell-1}(p)
corresponds to inserting 1 in the rightmost position in
\pi^{\prime}
, thereby creating a new right-to-left maximum. Clearly, no new occurrence of
p
is created, and no existing occurrence of
p
in
\pi^{\prime}
is affected.

•

\ell\cdot s_{n-1,k-1,\ell}(p)
counts the possibilities of picking one of
r_{i}
’s in
\pi^{\prime}
(in
\ell
ways) and inserting 1 just before it, that preserves the number of right-to-left maxima, creates an extra occurrence of
p
, and does not affect any other occurrences of
p
in
\pi^{\prime}
.

•

(n-1-\ell)\cdot s_{n-1,k,\ell}(p)
gives the remaining possibilities to insert 1: not at the end of
\pi^{\prime}
and not next to an
r_{i}
to the left. In each case, because of the shaded square (1,1), no new occurrence of
p
can be created (since
1r_{i}
cannot be an occurrence), and no other occurrence of
p
in
\pi^{\prime}
is affected.

The recurrence (
10
) is explained. The recurrence (
9
) now follows by observing that inserting the new minimal element immediately to the left of a right-to-left maximum creates a new occurrence of
p
, while inserting it in any other place does not change the number of occurrences of
p
; clearly, the number
i
of right-to-left maxima in
\pi^{\prime}\in S_{n-1}
is between 1 and
n-1
. Our proof is complete.
∎

.

Let
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
, and let
r_{1}>r_{2}>\cdots>r_{\ell}
be the sequence of right-to-left maxima in
\pi
, which define the areas
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{\ell}
as shown in Figure
7
. We define the set
M_{i}
as

M_{i}:=\{y\mid y\text{ is to the left of }r_{i-1}\text{ and }y<r_{i}\}.

By definition,
M_{1}=\emptyset
. Let the elements of
M_{i}
be ordered as
a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{j}
, where
j=|M_{i}|
. We also define the set
B_{i}:=A_{i}\setminus M_{i}
, so that
B_{i}
consists of all elements in
\pi
between
r_{i-1}
and
r_{i}
. Note that if
ab
is an occurrence of either pattern, then
b=r_{i}
for some
i
, and
a
must lie in
B_{i}
. Moreover, each
r_{i}
can be involved in multiple occurrences of the patterns

and

.

The transformation
f:\mathcal{S}_{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{n}
is obtained by considering
r_{i}
, for
i=\ell,\ell-1,\ldots,1
, one at a time.
For each
r_{i}
, we perform the following two steps sequentially:

•

Step 1 (First Complementation):
We apply a complementation to the elements in
A_{i}
. Specifically, we extract the values of these elements, reverse their relative order (i.e., mapping the minimum to the maximum, the second minimum to the second maximum, etc.), and assign them back to their original positions.

•

Step 2 (Second Complementation):
We apply a second complementation to the elements in
M_{i}
.

By sequentially applying Step 1 and Step 2 for each
r_{i},r_{i-1},\dots,r_{1}
, we obtain the transformed permutation
f(\pi)
.

In the example in Figure
9
, we consider
\pi=23784516\in S_{8}
. We apply the procedure as follows:
\pi=\uline{23}78\uline{451}6\xrightarrow{\text{complement }1,2,3,4,5}\uline{43}782156\xrightarrow{\text{complement }3,4}\uline{347}82156\xrightarrow{\text{complement }3,4,7}74382156=f(\pi)
. Note that the occurrences
28,38,78,46,56
of

in
\pi
are replaced by the occurrences

78,48,38,26,16
of

in
f(\pi)
.
Similarly, the occurrences
28
and
16
of

in
\pi
are replaced by the occurrences

78
and
58
of

in
f(\pi)
.

Figure 9:
The permutation
\pi=23784516
is mapped to
f(\pi)=74382156
under the bijection in the proof of Theorem
6.4
. The rectangles indicate the areas where complementation occurs.

We now justify that
f(\pi)=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{n}
only swaps the occurrences of

and

involving the set
B_{i}
. If
\pi_{j}r_{i}
is an occurrence of

, then there is no element to the left of
\pi_{j}
whose value lies between
\pi_{j}
and
r_{i}
. After the first complementation in
A_{i}
, the empty region above
\pi_{j}
to its left is transferred to the empty region below
\sigma_{j}
to its left, thereby turning the occurrence
\pi_{j}r_{i}
of

into an occurrence
\sigma_{j}r_{i}
of

. Similarly, any occurrence of

involving elements in
B_{i}
is transformed into an occurrence of

.

On the other hand, the second complementation ensures that the relative order of the elements in
M_{i}
in
\sigma
is the same as that in
M_{i}
in
\pi
. This implies that any occurrence of either pattern in
\pi
to the left of
r_{i-1}
remains an occurrence in
\sigma
to the left of
r_{i-1}
, and that no new occurrences of the patterns are created in this region.

Finally, it is clear that the complementation operations do not affect any occurrence of the patterns involving
r_{j}
for
j>i
, which completes the proof.
∎

7
Additional enumeration of avoiders

In this section, we mainly enumerate avoidance classes corresponding to some equivalence classes arising from trivial bijections, which are presented in Table
7
. However, we begin with two theorems that are not related to Table
7
.

Theorem 7.1
.

The g.f. for the number of permutations avoiding any pattern in Class
47
is

A(x)=\frac{F(x)}{1+x\sum_{n\geq 1}n!H_{n}x^{n}},

where
H_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k}
is the
n
-th Harmonic number.

A
a
b

Figure 10:
Related to the proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Proof.

We enumerate permutations avoiding the pattern

in Class 47. We claim that

F(x)=A(x)+A(x)\sum_{n\geq 2}(n-1)!H_{n-1}x^{n}.

(11)

Indeed, each permutation (counted by
F(x)
on the LHS) either avoids the pattern (and hence is counted by
A(x)
) or contains it. Among all occurrences
ab
of the pattern in
\pi
, select the one with the largest possible
b
, as shown in Figure
10
. Then the block
A
must avoid

, while the rest of the permutation is an arbitrary permutation with two left-to-right maxima (given by the elements
a
and
b
). It is well known
[
11
]
that permutations with
k
left-to-right maxima are in bijection with permutations with
k
cycles, so these permutations are counted by
c(n,2)
, the unsigned Stirling numbers of the first kind. Their ordinary g.f. is
\sum_{n\geq 2}(n-1)!H_{n-1}x^{n}
. This yields (
11
), and hence the formula for
A(x)
.
∎

Theorem 7.2
.

The g.f. for the number of permutations avoiding any pattern in Class
54
is

A(x)=F(x)-1-x-\frac{xF(x)}{1+xF(x)}\sum_{n\geq 1}n!H_{n}x^{n},

where
H_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k}
is the
n
-th Harmonic number.

in Class 54. We claim that for
n\geq 2
,

s_{n,0}(p)=n!-\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}[x^{i}]\frac{F(x)}{1+xF(x)}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1-i}k\cdot c(n-1-i,k).

(12)

Here,
[x^{i}]
denotes the coefficient of
x^{i}
in the subsequent generating function, and
c(n,k)
is the unsigned Stirling number of the first kind, counting permutations of length
n
with
k
right-to-left maxima.

We compute the number of
n
-permutations containing
p
and then subtract this from
n!
. Suppose that
ab
is an occurrence of
p
in
\pi\in S_{n}
, as shown in the left-hand diagram of Figure
7.2
, where we assume that
a
is chosen as small as possible. Then block
A
must avoid the pattern

, and if it contains
i
elements, then by Theorem
2.1
it can be formed in
[x^{i}]\frac{F(x)}{1+xF(x)}
ways. Since
b
is a right-to-left maximum in
\pi
, we can think of constructing
\pi
by appending the block
Aa
from the left, immediately below
b
, to a permutation of length
n-i-1
with
k
right-to-left maxima (where
b
is one of them). This construction is clearly reversible, so every such
\pi
is generated exactly once. Since
b
can be chosen in
k
ways, this completes the justification of (
12
), and the formula for
A(x)
follows from algebraic manipulations.

As for the pattern

, which is also in Class 54 but is not trivially Wilf-equivalent to the pattern
p
, essentially the same argument can be repeated using the right-hand diagram in Figure
7.2
, with
ba
now representing an occurrence of

and
b
playing the role of a left-to-right maximum. We therefore omit the further details.
∎

Theorem 7.4
.

For a pattern
p
in Classes
4
,

–

,

,

,

,

–

,

,

,

,

,

,

, and

,
s_{n,0}(p)=(n-1)!

.

Proof.

Avoidance for these classes was established in
[

]
, where various symmetries and the
Shading Lemma
were used to obtain all classes enumerated by

. Also, proofs for Classes 45 and 68 can be found in Theorem

and

]

.

Theorem 7.5
.

For a pattern
p
in Classes

,

,

,

,

, and

,
s_{n,0}(p)=n!/2
for

.

Proof.

Avoidance for these classes was established in
[
4
]
, where various symmetries and the Shading Lemma were used to obtain all classes enumerated by

. Also, the distributions for Classes 25, 33, and 63 were established by us in Theorems

,

, and

Theorem 7.6
.

For a pattern
p
in Classes
32
,
62
, and
84
, and
n\geq 2
,

s_{n,0}(p)=n!-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{(n-1)!}{i}.

Proof.

Avoidance for these classes was established in
[
4
, Prop. 24]
. The avoidance for Class 62 is also given in Theorem

, and the distribution for Class 32 is given in Theorem
3.6
.
∎

Theorem 7.7
.

,

, and

and

s_{n,0}(p)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}(n-k+1)\frac{n!}{k!}.

Proof.

Avoidance for these classes was established in

,

. The distribution for Class 61 is given in Theorem

.
∎

Theorem 7.8
.

For a pattern
p
in Classes
48
and
67
, we have
s_{n,0}(p)=n\cdot s_{n-1,0}(p)-s_{n-2,0}(p)
,
s_{-1,0}(p)=0
, and
s_{0,0}(p)=1
.

Proof.

Avoidance for these classes was established in
[
4
, Prop. 30 and 31]
.
∎

Theorem 7.9
.

For a pattern
p
in Classes
19
,
21
,
80
,
97
, and
103
, the g.f. is

A(x)=\frac{(1+x)F(x)}{1+xF(x)}.

Proof.

Avoidance for these classes was established in
[
4
, Prop. 23]
, where various symmetries and the Shading Lemma were used to obtain all classes enumerated by this sequence. The generating function was given by us in Theorem
3.16
, along with the full distribution for Class 80. The distribution for Class 103 was given in
[
5
]
.
∎

8
Concluding remarks

In this paper, we have developed a near-complete classification of equidistribution equivalence classes for mesh patterns of length 2, showing that their number lies between 105 and 108. Based on our results, we conjecture that the exact number is 105.

Conjecture 1
.

There are exactly
105
equivalence classes with respect to distribution for mesh patterns of length
2
.

As a consequence, we improved the known upper bound on the number of Wilf-classes from 56 to 49, reducing both the determination of the exact number of Wilf-classes and the complete classification of equidistribution classes to only three remaining conjectural cases listed in Table
5
, one of which is the following new conjecture:

Conjecture 2
.

Any two patterns belonging to the union of the following two sets are equivalent:

.

All bijective proofs presented in this paper establish the stronger property of joint equidistribution for the patterns under consideration. Nevertheless, a broader systematic study of joint equidistributions for mesh patterns of length 2 remains open and appears to be a promising direction for future research.

Finally, Table
6
highlights 19 remaining open avoidance problems and 72 open distribution problems. Further investigation of these unresolved cases would be both interesting and valuable for advancing our understanding of mesh pattern avoidance and distribution.
